<thead><tr><th>Original question</th><th>Skill-informed rewrite</th></tr></thead>
<tbody><tr>
<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> Which chemical transformation best describes the catalytic activity of this protein?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> D-glucosamine 1-phosphate + acetyl-CoA → N-acetyl-D-glucosamine 1-phosphate + CoA + H⁺<br><br><strong>B.</strong> D-glucosamine 6-phosphate + acetyl-CoA → N-acetyl-D-glucosamine 6-phosphate + CoA + H⁺<br><br><strong>C.</strong> N-acetyl-D-glucosamine 6-phosphate + H₂O → D-glucosamine 6-phosphate + acetate<br><br><strong>D.</strong> D-glucosamine + acetyl-CoA → N-acetyl-D-glucosamine + CoA + H⁺<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: Analyzing the protein sequence reveals several key features characteristic of a GNAT (GCN5-related N-acetyltransferase) family enzyme. The most diagnostic motif is &quot;IHGAGSRGR&quot; which contains the conserved &quot;GxG/A&quot; motif essential for acetyl-CoA binding in GNAT fold enzymes. Additionally, the sequence contains motifs like &quot;IEDSNS&quot; and &quot;KRFEAMR&quot; that are characteristic of GlmM (glucosamine-6-phosphate N-acetyltransferase). The protein length (~220 amino acids) is consistent with GlmM enzymes. In the hexosamine biosynthetic pathway, GlmM catalyzes the acetylation of glucosamine-6-phosphate using acetyl-CoA as the acetyl donor, producing N-acetyl-glucosamine-6-phosphate. This corresponds to choice B. Choice A is incorrect because it involves the 1-phosphate position (not the physiologically relevant position for GlmM). The WAP domain (also called the &quot;elafin-like&quot; domain) is characterized by 8 conserved cysteines. The sequence context with a signal peptide followed by a WAP-like domain strongly suggests this is a **serine-type endopeptidase inhibitor**.

The question specifically mentions &quot;the N-terminal hydrophobic stretch and cysteine-rich region&quot; and asks about &quot;family-level interpretation.&quot;

Let me consider the options:
A. Cysteine-type endopeptidase inhibitor activity - This would be for cysteine protease inhibitors (like cystatins). Cystatins have a different cysteine pattern (typically fewer cysteines, around 2-3).
B. Serine-type endopeptidase activity - This would mean the protein IS a serine protease, not an inhibitor. The sequence doesn&#x27;t show the catalytic triad of a serine protease.
C. Peptidase activity - General peptidase activity. Doesn&#x27;t fit the sequence architecture.
D. Serine-type endopeptidase inhibitor activity - This fits perfectly with a WAP domain or Kunitz-like domain protein that inhibits serine proteases.

The 8-cysteine pattern is the hallmark of the WAP/four-disulfide core family, which is well-known for inhibiting serine proteases (particularly neutrophil elastase and cathepsin G). The signal peptide indicates it&#x27;s secreted, consistent with a protease inhibitor function.

The answer is D.
